# Plato — Progress Tracker

This file is the running record of the project. Every merged change gets
a row in the change log. Any developer or agent can reconstruct the
state of the project from this file plus `git log`.

## How to keep this file correct

1. Add a change-log row (date, commit hash or "this commit", area,
   summary, how verified) for every meaningful change, before you push.
2. Flip a milestone status only after its demo passes. Say how it was
   verified, with evidence (command output, screenshot, transcript).
3. Never delete rows. Corrections go in a new row.
4. Keep the "Current focus" line accurate whenever you stop working.

Current focus: **M4 IS DONE (2026-09-27) — its two deliverables are complete and both are measured: the acceptance test is restated AND executed (7 of 7 clauses, `node scripts/field/acceptance.mjs --run` over its harness cases in real VS Code), and the secret audit is complete (D49au).** The remaining work is **CHOSEN capability work, not the next phase**: the M5 plan is **complete through Phase 6** (D49a–ah) and **Phase 7 is recorded-not-built by design** — its first item, subagents, amends the one-worker lock (D45a-3) and needs an explicit amendment, not a slip-in. **Tier C — the ordered capability list — is COMPLETE: 1 (editor diagnostics, D49av) DONE · 2 (bound `auto` by repetition, D49aw) DONE · 3 (undo) BUILT AND VERIFIED END TO END (D49az, 2026-09-28; decided D49ax the day before).** Undo turned out **not** to change what an "Allow" means, as HANDOFF had predicted — the write still happens as approved; what changes is what a *mistake* costs — and it turned out **small**, because the old bytes are already read at the write site. Building it settled three things D49ax had left open, and each one is load-bearing: **the copy is taken at the executor, not at the approval preview** (in `auto` no preview is built, so the preview would have been the one place that never ran in the mode where undo matters most); **the plan is remade at the click, never held from the card** (a user who edits a file between reading the card and clicking it would otherwise have the old copy put back over their work — Kilo Code's bug, through a different door); and **a file Plato created is undone by deleting it**, guarded by the same fingerprint and performed last. **Also shipped with it (D49ay): old run transcripts are compressed after 15 days and never deleted on a timer, and the History page lets the user delete one run or all of them.** **Newest: a protected file is now READABLE with its values replaced by a placeholder (D49bb, 2026-09-28)** — the refusal fired on the ACCESS, and the access is not the dangerous act; the guard moved to where the secret MOVES, `shell` is the one place the old refusal stays, and the protected list became the owner's (`plato.secrets.protect` / `plato.secrets.allow`). **The three follow-ups D49bb left open are all BUILT (D49bc, 2026-09-28)** and were the last thing on this list: the in-panel **Protected files** section (D45c), the **card** for the text file the masker cannot read, and **seeding the secret map** from the workspace so an indirect `cat *.properties` in `auto` is covered — so `cat ~/.aws/credentials` outside the workspace is now the only boundary left, stated rather than implied. **Newest (D49bd, 2026-10-01): a question's tab is NAMED, not numbered** — the model writes a one- or two-word `header` per question (`Contract`, `Test runner`, `Cleanup`), the tab shows it, a question the model left unlabelled keeps its number, and the strip wraps rather than clipping when the names are wider than the panel. **Newest (D49be + D49bf, 2026-10-06): the secret check reads a shell command the way bash reads it** — `cat key'store'.properties` was refused by nothing, and now the D49aq tokenizer puts the name back together before the deny-list looks at it — **and the model's context window comes from the CONNECTION alone.** The shipped catalog table is deleted rather than ignored, so it cannot rot or creep back, and when the connection advertises nothing the assumption is **128k, up from the 8,192 that used to fold a good session after three files**. The trade that bought is recorded rather than glossed: a model that advertises nothing and truly holds less than 128k now gets a 400 instead of an early fold. **What is next is the founder's to choose, not the queue's:** Tier C held three items and all three are done, so the remaining work is either the deferred capability list (the two guard proposals, which need decisions first; `web_search`, which needs a backend decision; subagents, which amend the one-worker lock D45a-3) or the smaller recorded defects. Nothing is blocked. Also settled and built at his direction: **three modes — `plan` / `edit` / `auto`** with `edit` absorbing `act` (D49ak), **`auto` fetches without asking** (D49ak, amending D49p), and **a question may be multi-select** (D49al). **Open and waiting on the founder:** the two compaction items the repairs left (both measured, neither the cause of what he was seeing), the model-list ordering he asked for, and the deferred defect that an **unknown model's context window floors at 8,192**, so the compaction guard fires at ~6.5k tokens. **The publish gate (trademark D16, license D19) is his and comes last** — the repo stays private and no license text is added before D19.

## Deferred / backlog (do not lose these)

- **CLOSED — BUILT (2026-09-28): the existing-copy audit (D49ba).** The founder's answer to the
  audited list was *"convert all while we are at it, doesn't hurt, does it?"* — and it hurt in one
  specific way, which is now recorded: **the copy was a contract.** The field harness detected every
  approval card by matching the sentence `Approve <tool>?` and read the tool out of it, so a reworded
  heading would have failed twenty cases at once and read as a product regression. The card is now
  found by its class and its tool by `data-tool` — stable things — and the copy is free to improve.
  **Everything outstanding is done:** the approval heading (`Let Plato edit src/a.ts?`), its session
  button (`Allow every edit this session`), the guard line (`Plato did not run the write. That file is
  protected.`), the activity row (`12 files read · 2 commands run`), the progress line, the three
  session-approval notices, the `/compact` blurb, the orchestration chip, the empty-history line, the
  compaction note, and undo's write failures. One tool-word table in core serves all three places
  that used to print identifiers, and a test asserts no entry contains its own key.
  **Two audit suggestions were not taken, and both are recorded in D49ba:** "Keep going" keeps its two
  words (the card carries the second effect, because a two-idea label breaks the same rule), and the
  session notices were simplified rather than given a per-tool noun table.
  **The sweep introduced a bug, and the render suite caught it within the hour:** the approval card's
  "allow every … this session" threw a `ReferenceError`, and neither handler marked the card answered
  — which would have left a spent card reading as live.

- **A per-project command allow/reject list — DEFERRED by the founder (2026-09-24).** The
  follow-up offered for the remaining prompt: `npm test`, `./gradlew`, `node` and every run script
  still ask in `edit` (a build file executes arbitrary code, so it cannot be granted from the
  command text). The proposed fix was a `PLATO.md` field listing exact commands the user permits.
  **His reason for deferring, recorded because it is the whole objection:** *"per project allow,
  reject list is too broad scope and can't be exhaustively covered"* — a list that covers most of a
  project's commands invites the belief that it covers all of them, and the gaps are invisible.
  **Do not reopen this by adding a list without answering that objection.** The honest alternatives
  if it does bite: leave it (`auto` already runs everything unasked), or grant one command shape at
  a time as a named, narrow decision like the read-only allowlist (D49an).
- **CLOSED — BUILT (2026-09-24): the action model is three modes — `plan` / `edit` / `auto`
  (D49ak).** The founder raised it as *"I cannot properly differentiate between act and
  edit"*, and he was right: old `edit` was quiet about writes but **denied the shell**, and
  old `act` had the shell but **asked on every single write**. **`edit` absorbed `act`** and
  now reads freely, asks for a write, remembers a yes **per file for the session**, and asks
  for every command — strictly better than either mode it replaces. **The one part NOT built
  as he stated it: "run scripts but change no project files" is not enforceable**, because a
  host can classify a *tool* and not a *command*, and sorting commands by their text is
  **the exact mechanism that failed in D49ai**. So `plan` was left with no shell at first; **the
  enforceable alternative turned out to be a command ALLOWLIST, and it is now built** — `plan` and
  `edit` both run read-only commands without a card (D49an, D49ao). **What
  he actually wants from `plan` is the missing `web_search`** — `web_fetch` already
  asks-and-works there. **`auto` now fetches without asking** (amending D49p), which is his
  call and is recorded with its cost in D49ak. Full reasoning, the falsification and the test
  gap this closed are in **D49ak** — read that, not this line.
- **CLOSED — BUILT (2026-09-24): a question can be multi-select (D49al).** The founder raised
  it as *"a model can reason to ask a multiple choice answer, so we should support
  checkboxes"* — and the reason is the design, so the feature is not a nicety: a model
  that can see several correct answers and may offer only one makes the user discard the
  rest. **A question now carries an optional `multiple` flag**; without it nothing changed.
  The picks come back as **one string per question**, `"; "`-joined, so the transport to the
  model is untouched, and the tool result says `Answers:` rather than `Answer:` so the model
  can tell the kinds apart. **The option limit was never a multi-select
  rule:** the schema lets the model declare the labels, single and multi alike, and the card adds
  the free-text *Others* row ON TOP of them (3 declared labels show as 3 boxes + 1 text row).
  **How many boxes may be TICKED is unlimited.** What was left out is only a one-click "tick
  everything" and a minimum-picks rule. **The label ceiling is now 3 and the question ceiling is
  4, on the founder's instruction — see D49am.** Read **D49al** for the reasoning.

- **CLOSED as NOT a defect (2026-09-24): the compaction threshold is already a percentage
  of the model's own window — and that is the right shape.** A previous session queued
  *"the threshold should be an absolute token window, not a percentage"*, copying Claude
  Code's design. **The founder rejected the premise and the code agrees with him:**
  `shouldAutoCompact` is `estimatedTokens >= (maxContext ?? DEFAULT_CONTEXT_WINDOW) *
  threshold`, with `threshold` defaulting to **0.8** and clamped to `[0.5, 0.95]` — so
  **nothing is hardcoded**, and the slider already reads *"80% of context"*. A percentage
  **scales with the model**: 256k trips at 204,800, 512k at 409,600, and the founder's
  reported 1,000,000 at 800,000. A fixed token count would be the thing that breaks across
  models. **The 800,000 figure was never a constant** — it is 80% of *his* window, and
  reading it back as a hardcoded guard is the mistake this entry exists to stop.
  **What has since changed, and does not touch this argument:** the *unknown* case used to
  fall at 8,192 and trip at ~6,553 tokens. **D49bf (2026-10-06) raised that assumption to
  128k and removed the catalog entirely**, and the percentage shape this entry defends is
  untouched — only the number it is a percentage *of*, when nothing knows it.
- **Trim old tool OUTPUT before summarising anything (measured 2026-09-24, deferred —
  lower priority than it looked).** Tool results are the overwhelming majority of a
  request — **80.8%** on the founder's 78-turn run — and both Claude Code and Codex run a
  cheap snip/microcompact pass before the expensive summariser, so the theory was that
  folding could usually be avoided instead of performed. **The measurement says it would
  not have helped him:** his runs end around 11% of his window, so the guard never trips
  and there was no summarising to avoid (his transcript holds **zero `context-compacted`
  events**). **What was actually wrong was that the log had no record of tool activity at
  all — that is D49ag, and it is done.** This item stays for models with a **small or
  unknown** window, where the guard does fire early; it is not a repair for anything the
  founder has seen.
- **The fold's cadence constants count MESSAGES, not pairs, and their names say
  otherwise (found 2026-09-24, recorded not changed).** `AUTO_FOLD_AT = 26` and
  `KEEP_AFTER_FOLD = 12` are written and discussed as *pairs*, but `tailPairs` counts
  every `you`/`bot` row — so a fold fires on roughly **13 exchanges** and keeps about
  **6**, twice as often as the names suggest. Not a defect on its own; it means the fold
  runs more often than anyone reading the constants would guess, which matters when
  reasoning about how much a summary carries. Renaming is the cheap fix; changing the
  numbers is a behavioural decision and wants the founder.
- ~~**How to bound `auto` now that no turn cap exists (opened 2026-09-24, deliberately not
  solved).**~~ **CLOSED — BUILT as D49aw (2026-09-27).** D49ab removed `MAX_TURNS` from every
  mode. In `plan`/`edit` that is plainly right — an approval card gates every write, so the
  user IS the backstop. In `auto` there is no click in the loop, so **nothing stopped a model
  that kept calling tools without concluding.** The founder's own view, recorded at the time
  because it is why this was a backlog item and not a repair: *models conclude their tasks
  anyway*, and the runway fear may be exaggerated — Claude Code ships an auto mode with no
  turn limit. **What a bound should NOT be, learned the expensive way:** a step count. D49ab
  removed one that had been applied to every mode and cut a *working* run at two-thirds after
  the user had approved eleven writes; and turns measure the wrong thing — that run spent
  10,442 output tokens across 40 turns, while a thinking run can spend more in three. **A
  user-set `$/turns` budget was built once and removed the same day as an unrequested
  capability (D49h) — do not re-propose it as the answer without reading that entry.**
  **The shape predicted here is the shape that shipped:** the failure mode is **repetition**,
  not volume — the same call returning the same result. The bound is now built with two
  triggers, a nudge at 3 and a card at 5, and it **resets on any success** so it caps wasted
  effort rather than progress. **The structural point still stands:** `duo`/`academy` put a
  supervising observer in the loop, and the nudge rides that same channel, so an `auto` run
  under an observer is supervised where a solo one is only bounded.
- **Ordering the model list, so the user controls picker priority (founder-requested
  2026-09-23, deliberately held back).** D49z deleted the tick boxes, so a connection's
  model list is now the fetched list in stored order, and the chat picker shows that
  order. The founder wants to control it: **drag to reorder**, or up/down arrows, with the
  order shown in the picker. **Why it is worth more than cosmetics:** when the chosen
  model stops being offered, `pickEffectiveModel` falls back to the first available model
  — so the top of the user's list becomes their stated default, which answers a question
  that already exists. **Four things to settle before building it:** (a) drag-only
  reordering has **no keyboard path**, which is wrong for a keyboard-first product, so
  ship arrows or row buttons with it; (b) HTML5 drag-and-drop is **hard to drive from the
  render harness**, so the order logic (*move i to j*, and *merge a refreshed list into
  the user's order*) belongs in core as tested pure functions, leaving a thin
  manually-verified gesture; (c) it is a **capability, not a repair** — do not fold it
  into a fix; (d) it does not scale — dragging is for five to fifteen models, and on a
  provider returning hundreds the search box is the real answer. **Merge rule when it
  lands:** keep the user's order for models still offered, append new ones at the END
  (never insert by rank), and do not remember the position of a model that disappeared.
- **Plato never reads a reasoning model's thinking tokens (found by the second real
  run, 2026-09-22).** The OpenAI-compatible adapter reads `delta.content` and nothing
  else, so `reasoning_content` — the field DeepSeek-R1-class models put their thinking
  in — is **discarded entirely**. Three consequences, in increasing order of importance:
  the model's reasoning is invisible to the user; those tokens are billed as output but
  never shown; and a turn that spends its whole budget on reasoning arrives as an
  **empty** turn, which is the condition that triggered D49s. **Not fixed, and not
  obviously a bug to fix** — streaming someone's raw chain-of-thought into the panel is
  a product decision (Claude Code shows a collapsed thinking block; some tools show
  nothing), and reading the field does not by itself decide what to do with it. What is
  clearly wrong today is that the tokens are counted and then thrown away with no note.
  **Do not build a thinking display without the founder.**
- **The context-window fallback only helps when the connection reports one (D49t,
  2026-09-22).** Steps 1–3 of the founder's plan are built: the discovered numbers now
  reach the RUN (not just the header), discovery happens automatically before a run, and
  the missing fields are read — vLLM's `max_model_len`, and LiteLLM's `/model/info`,
  which is the only place LiteLLM publishes sizes. **What still reaches the default:**
  a provider that reports nothing (plain OpenAI's own `/models` carries no window at all),
  an endpoint that refuses `/model/info`, and any connection where discovery simply fails.
  **Since D49bf the default is 128k rather than 8,192**, and the catalog supplies nothing —
  so this list is now the complete set of inputs, with no table behind it.
  **Two unbuilt options remain the founder's call:** (4) a per-connection window the user
  declares, which is the only input that is correct by construction rather than discovered
  — and would want a settings screen; (5) **Ollama and llama.cpp**, whose windows are
  runtime values on different endpoints (`num_ctx` in `/api/show`'s parameters, or the
  served value in `/api/ps`; `n_ctx` in `/props`). **Ollama is the cautionary case:** it
  advertises its TRAINING maximum — often 128k — while serving a fraction of it, so its
  reported number must never be taken at face value. Nothing here needs building until
  Plato is pointed at a setup discovery cannot read.
- **CLOSED (D49bf, 2026-10-06): a model outside the catalog was assumed to hold only
  8,192 tokens.** Found by the first real run (2026-09-22): `maxContextFor` returned `null`
  for a name it did not know, `shouldAutoCompact` fell back to `UNKNOWN_CONTEXT_FLOOR` =
  8,192, and compaction tripped at ~6,553 tokens — a LiteLLM group called `Auto-Act` plus
  three Java files and a few tool results were enough. **The founder chose the third of the
  three options this item listed.** The window now comes from the connection (`/models`),
  the shipped catalog no longer claims one at all, and the assumption when nothing is
  advertised is **128k**. The trade that bought — a model that advertises nothing and truly
  holds less than 128k gets a **400** rather than an early fold — is recorded in D49bf,
  along with what repair looks like when it happens (refresh the model list). **The
  per-connection field and the Ollama/llama.cpp runtime reads above remain unbuilt**, for
  the same reason as before: nothing needs them until Plato is pointed at a setup discovery
  cannot read.
- **Image thumbnails in the attachment chip (deferred in D49o, with a named
  prerequisite).** The chip is a glyph plus the size because a thumbnail would
  mean posting the whole file to the webview as a `data:` URI — **there is no
  resize step anywhere in the pipeline**, so a 3 MB screenshot becomes a ~4 MB
  message to paint a 16px box. It would also need `img-src data:` in a CSP that is
  currently `default-src 'none'` with no image source at all. **The prerequisite is
  a resize step** (or accepting the wide grant knowingly). Not a bug — the name
  and size are on the chip — but a smaller thumbnail is the nicer affordance, and
  this is the note so it is a decision rather than an oversight.
- **The founder could not state what Plato's modes mean (founder, 2026-09-20) — HALF
  ANSWERED, and the half that remains is the naming, not the behaviour (D49ak,
  2026-09-24).** His words were *"I don't fully understand the four modes Plato offer,
  edit, auto, plan etc."* **What D49ak settled: there are three modes, not four** — `act`
  was merged into `edit` — so the specific confusion between two modes that behaved almost
  identically is gone, and with it two of the four stale copy lines. **What is NOT settled,
  and is the same complaint:** **"mode" still means two different things in this product.**
  1. **Orchestration modes** — Solo / Duo / Academy, in *Settings → Orchestration*. Who is
     watching: 0 / 1 / N philosophers.
  2. **Permission modes** — plan / edit / auto, in the composer popover under the mode
     button. What Plato is allowed to do.
  Neither surface disambiguates, and they still differ in presentation as well as
  vocabulary: the permission button prints the **bare lowercase id** (`lbl.textContent = m`),
  so it reads `edit` — a bare verb — while the orchestration cards read *Solo / Duo /
  Academy*, proper names with a sentence underneath. **Current permission copy, verbatim, so
  the discussion starts from the real text — this is the copy D49ak wrote:**
  - **plan** — "Read-only. Plato can look at files and answer questions, but never modifies
    anything on disk or runs shell."
  - **edit** — "Writes and commands, each behind an approval card. A file you approve once is
    not asked about again this session."
  - **auto** — "No approval clicks, web requests included. Plato runs end-to-end on its own —
    watch it, and stop it if it goes wrong."
  Directions to weigh when it is discussed — **not chosen, not built**: give one family a
  different word so only one thing is a "mode"; label the permission modes with a noun the
  user already has ("Ask first" / "Just do it") rather than a bare verb; or present both
  axes together in one place ("who watches: Solo · what Plato may do: Ask"). **`plan|edit|auto`
  is also the `Mode` type and the `decide()` table — do not rename names or ids without the
  discussion.** **Corrected here (2026-09-24): this entry used to call the mode "a persisted
  setting" and it is not one** — the mode lives in a field on the chat host and resets to
  `edit` every session, which is also why D49ak needed no settings migration.
- **A retry notice never reaches the screen while the run is in progress (found,
  2026-09-17, D49j).** D49f promised that *"every retry is announced — 'rate
  limited, retrying in 4s' — so a stalled turn is explained rather than looking
  hung."* The notice **is** emitted, but the extension's bus handler for
  `notice` only calls `push()`, which writes the host's persisted log — there is
  **no `send()`**, and `push()` never posts to the webview. So the announcement
  appears only after a **reload** replays the log. Bus notices are log-only;
  `this.send('notice', …)` is used only by host-side code paths (observer,
  commands), never by the bus bridge. **Not fixed here on purpose:** posting a
  notice mid-stream is not a one-line change, because `addMsg` appends to `#log`
  while the live `#streaming` element is the *last* child — a notice would land
  **after** it and subsequent `md` updates would then render the reply **above**
  the notice, re-creating exactly the misordering the chronological-segmentation
  work fixed. Doing it properly means committing the live element first (what
  `turn` does), which interacts with the reading-experience item above. One line
  to change, real design underneath: sequence it with the rendering work.
- **Reading experience (founder, 2026-08-26):** agent narration ("Let me examine…")
  and the actual answer to the user are jumbled together in one stream. Needs
  visual separation of progress-narration vs. delivered information — paragraph
  spacing, distinct styling per message kind, in the spirit of Cline/Claude Code
  but with Plato's own formatting. Related structural item: the host streams one
  cumulative markdown blob into a fixed `#streaming` element, so narration and
  tool activity cannot interleave; needs host-side reply segmentation.
  **Phase 2 (streaming, D49) rewrites exactly this path** — the host's
  cumulative-blob `md` posting. Do them together, or sequence them deliberately
  and say which came first; changing the transport underneath an unresolved
  rendering problem is how the problem gets re-diagnosed from scratch.
  **Sequenced 2026-09-17: the transport went FIRST (D49j).** The cumulative-blob
  model is unchanged — same `#streaming` element, same replace-wholesale
  `streamHtml` — so this item is still entirely open, and the unit of work it
  implies is now better defined than it was: the host has a `replyBuffer`
  (committed blocks) and a `streamPreview` (the turn in flight), and a commitment
  supersedes the preview. Separating narration from the delivered answer means
  splitting one of those two, not inventing a third channel.
- **Mermaid diagram rendering (founder, 2026-08-26):** Plato emits mermaid fences
  (architecture diagrams); the renderer shows them as labeled code blocks, not
  rendered diagrams. Needs its own decision: bundle mermaid.js (~1MB, breaks the
  renderer's zero-dependency doctrine, SVG XSS surface inside the escape-first
  webview, CSP must allow the bundled lib) vs. render-on-demand vs. status quo.
  Not a small change — decide before building.
- ~~Philosopher cost metering~~ RESOLVED 2026-08-26 (0.9.5): ObserverAgent
  accumulates tokens and prices each check via `estimateCost`; done card shows
  `· ≈$N.NNNN watching`.
- ~~Observer digest is thin~~ RESOLVED 2026-08-26 (0.9.2 arg hints + recent-
  reply tail) — validated by trial #4 where Diogenes caught no-op diff
  thrashing and analysis paralysis mid-run (5 interventions).
- ~~Auto-continuation of turns truncated by the token cap~~ **STALE — corrected
  2026-09-17 (D49 clean-up).** The item said *"stopReason is now surfaced; the
  loop still just stops with a note."* That stopped being true at **D25f**: the
  loop marks a no-tool turn truncated, pushes a *"Continue exactly where you left
  off"* user turn and `continue`s, bounded to **one continuation per run**
  (`autoContinued`, `loop.ts`). The cap note is now the **fallback** for a second
  truncation, not the behaviour — so the note is what you see when the retry
  *also* got cut, which is a different thing from what this item described. Two
  loop tests pin both halves (`loop.test.ts`). Removed from the backlog. Same
  class as the `.msg.tool` item above: a status written once and never re-derived.
- Add-connection transition animation (founder-requested, deferred twice).
- ~~Dead `.msg.tool` CSS rules in webview~~ **WRONG — corrected 2026-09-17 (D48).**
  The class is **live**: `webview.ts:1970` still emits `el('div', 'msg tool')`, and
  `.msg.tool-summary` at line 3613 is emitted too. Both have rules and both are
  used; the item was stale, not the CSS. Removed from the backlog.
- OpenRouter authoritative `usage.cost` passthrough (still absent —
  `core/src/providers/` has no `usage.cost` handling today).
- **M4 closeout items** — the acceptance test and the secret audit. **DONE (D49at, D49au);
  the clause-by-clause record is in HANDOFF → "M4 closeout — the stale blueprint clauses,
  and what closed them"**, not duplicated here. **The
  trademark sweep (D16) and the license decision (D19) are NOT M4** — they left it
  on 2026-09-25 by the founder's call ("we are still in dev") and are now the
  **publish gate**: last, and only before a release is actually planned. See
  HANDOFF → "The publish gate".

| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bo, amended) The queue's guard was `running`, and `running` is set too late — so a `/queue` typed while the host prepared a run started a SECOND run alongside the first.** The rule was right and the guard was wrong. `this.running` is set at the END of a run's preparation — after the key is read, the model pool filled and MCP servers started, a span that allows twenty seconds — so a `/queue` arriving in that window found it false, drained the queue and started a run concurrently with the one still being prepared. Two runs then shared one controller, one webview and one runs directory. **The diagnosis came from evidence rather than from guessing, which is why one diagnostic was worth leaving in place after the earlier flake:** the single failure reported the queued run's task as the FIRST prompt's — only possible if the queued run STARTED earlier — and the case took two minutes where it takes thirty-five seconds. `runInFlight` now spans start-to-unwind, set in `startRun` and cleared in its promise's `finally` so it clears on EVERY exit, including the eight guard clauses that return early from `run()` — which is why the queue's advance moved out of the body and into that chain, carrying `lastRunEnd` out with it because the advance can no longer see the local that held it. **This is the same pre-run window D49bm found for Pause and Cancel, and the queue was the case left uncovered** — the durable lesson is the shape of the miss: when a hazard is found in one control, the others reaching the same state want looking at in the same pass. **Proving it needed a way INTO the window, and the stub has one now:** `modelsDelayMs` stalls the model-discovery call the host makes while preparing, turning a millisecond window into a four-second one, and a solo `FIELD_CASES=26` is the run whose pool is stale so the stall applies. **Against the pre-fix source that run fails with the overlap printed — the queued run started 2.7 seconds before the first run's last write.** **And one assertion written for this was DEMOTED after being tested against the broken build:** comparing the two transcripts' header stamps passed against the defect, because `startedAt` is written when a run reaches the wire, after its own preparation, so two runs started into one window stamp in either order. The check that catches it reads the two runs' OVERLAP — the queued run's `startedAt` against the first transcript's last-write time. | **839 unit tests**, unchanged · render **23 suites / 653 assertions**, unchanged · **stub field 28 of 28** · **real-model case 30 passed 6 of 6 after the fix** (it was 1 failure in 10 before), case 31 unchanged · **acceptance 8 of 8 clauses** · **falsification by reverting to the pre-fix `extension.ts` (commit `9fc7472`), which fails case 26 on the overlap assertion AND on the chip assertion, then green with the fix restored** — both checks have teeth, which is exactly what the demoted one did not. |
| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bo) `/queue`: a follow-up can be held for the next run.** The founder's read was that **steering already exists — and he was right, so `/steer` was NOT built.** Typing while a run is active already posts guidance, which queues FIFO and is injected into the model's next turn; a command for it would be a rename. **`/queue` is the genuinely missing thing, and the gap is that one channel carries two meanings:** while a run is active Enter means *change this run*, while idle it means *start a task*, so there is no way to say **"when you are done, do this next"** — a follow-up typed mid-run is injected into the current run, where the model may blend the two or drop the first. Checked against the field before cutting: Claude Code ships exactly this with three priorities (send now / after the current step / after the whole reply as a new turn) and Plato had working equivalents of the first two. **The advance rule is narrower than the obvious one and lives in core so it can be falsified:** `nextQueued(queue, reason)` hands the head on only for `complete` and `cap`, because **"not cancelled" was the first version and it is not enough** — an **errored** run would burn the whole queue against a dead provider one run at a time, and a **stuck** run would drain it into the loop the model could not escape. The hold is sticky until the user asks for the queue to continue, so one cancel cannot be quietly undone by the next run that ends cleanly. **`/queue` is the first command that carries TEXT**, which needed small plumbing (the webview kept only the command token), and the typed path and the menu-picked path share one payload rule — **not tidiness: typing `/queue fix the tests` opens the command menu, so Enter runs the highlighted row, and an earlier version sent that row empty, dropping the message and answering with the usage note.** The field driver found that by typing the way a person does, before the field run existed. The queue is **in memory only**: a reload kills the run too, and a persisted non-empty queue would either sit forever or **start a run the moment the panel opened**. The chips get their **own row**, not the attachment row, because an attachment is consumed by the send while a queued message outlives runs; removal posts the **index** and is not applied locally, so the view cannot take out the wrong message. | **839 unit tests** (+14: `nextQueued` over all five endings, plus the park tests) · render **23 suites / 653 assertions** (+39: a new `queue` suite and the pause block) · field **stub 28 of 28 cases pass** and **real-model 30 and 31 pass** (DeepSeek; the key is read from the founder's file and never printed) · **acceptance 8 of 8 clauses**, clause 8 added: *"The run controls mean what they say"* · four falsifications, each reverted and re-run green: draining on cancel fails the hold test; letting the view edit the queue locally fails a render assertion; dropping the payload rule fails the typed `/queue` assertion; and removing the `delayMs` window makes the pause cases unrunnable rather than passing. |
| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bn) Closing the chat panel stops the run.** The panel IS the run's supervisor — the buttons, the cards and the log all live in it — and closing it did nothing: `onDidDispose` archived the chat and tore down the watchers and **never touched the controller**, so the run kept calling tools and writing files with no UI, no Pause, no Cancel, and **no timeout on a parked card**. The first write in `edit` then parked forever on a card nobody could answer, and in `auto` there are no cards at all, so nothing would even ask. **The founder chose "stop the run" over "keep it running in the background", and the reasoning is that nothing in the code ever claimed a run survives the tab.** `dispose()` now sets `disposed` first, releases parked cards and cancels, then **awaits the run promise before archiving** — the cancelled run pushes its last rows and closes its writer on the way out, so archiving first would persist a log about to change. **The record of WHY is written by the run in its `finally`, and that placement is load-bearing:** a run still being prepared has no bus yet, and a bus notice emitted before the first `run-started` is **dropped for having no file to land in** — exactly the silence this is about. Building the field case found **the sharpest defect of the three: `dispose()` never reached its `cancel()`**, because `releaseParkedCards()` posts to a webview that is already gone and **the throw abandoned the teardown half-done**. The symptom was a transcript carrying the panel-close notice **next to** `run-completed {reason: 'complete'}` — the run saying its supervisor had closed and then reporting it finished normally. | part of the **23 suites / 653 assertions** and the **28 of 28** stub cases above · **field case 28** closes the real tab and asserts `run-completed {reason: 'cancelled'}` with the panel-close notice in the record · **two falsifications, each reverted and re-run green:** rethrowing from `send()` fails case 28 on exactly the defect above, and removing the `disposed` cancel leaves the run `complete`. **A HARNESS LIMIT is recorded rather than papered over:** case 28 asserts from the **run record only** — the harness cannot step back inside a webview panel it has closed, so the on-screen half is not checked and the spec skips the screen read for that one case **by name**; the record is the right witness anyway, since the same bus notice writes the chat row. Case 28 must stay LAST. Two harness routes were tried and **both failed in a full-suite run while working in isolation** — `closeEditor('Plato Chat')` once the tab bar was crowded, and the command palette, which never became visible that late — so the tab's close button is now clicked in the workbench DOM. |
| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bm) Pause says what is true, and the run record says it too.** The founder's audit question was *"do they really mean what they claim"*, and for Pause the answer was no: `pause()` set a flag the loop reads in **exactly two places** while the button flipped its own label to *Resume* **on the click** — so between the click and the next safe point the control claimed the run had stopped while only a REQUEST was recorded, and when a turn returned no tool calls the run ended without ever reaching a gate, so the pause was claimed and never happened. **It left no record anywhere**, and **no field case had ever clicked it**. The design follows from one fact — **only `gate()` knows the run has stopped** — so `RunController` gained an optional `onPark`, fired from `gate()` under three rules that are each a bug avoided: **edge-triggered** (or every ordinary gate reports a resume that never happened); **`true` on ENTERING the wait**, so a resume-then-pause inside one wait cannot claim the run was never parked; and **isolated with a `try`/`catch` exactly as `AgentBus.emit` is** — without it a throw from the UI callback propagates out of the `await` and **kills the run**. The UI is three states from two different facts: `pauseWanted` (the view's, at once) and `pauseParked` (the host's, never guessed), so the label reads `Pause` → `Pausing…` → `Resume`, clicking `Pausing…` takes the request back, and the status line says `Paused` only while the run is genuinely stopped. The pause that never lands is named — *"The run finished before the pause took effect."* — rather than swallowed. **Two more defects came out of the same window, the seconds between the view showing its buttons and the host reaching `running = true`**: the view's controls appear the instant `ask` is posted, but the host reads the key, fills the model pool and starts MCP servers first, **so a pause clicked in that window was dropped in silence — and so was a cancel, which is worse, because losing a cancel means Plato starts the run the user just asked it to stop.** Both are now held and applied when the run exists; a pending cancel means the run never starts. **A third defect no render suite could have seen:** `send()` builds `{type, text, …meta}`, so the host sent `m.text.parked` while the view read `m.parked` — **the render suite passed because it fakes the host and posted the shape the view expected**, and the field case caught it through the label never leaving `Pausing…`; the suite now posts the host's real shape. | part of the **839 unit tests**, the **23 suites / 653 assertions** and the **28 of 28** stub cases above · **field case 24** clicks Pause on a run the stub holds open with a new per-step `delayMs` (a stub with no delay finishes in microseconds, so there was no window to click in), asserts the label is `Pausing…` and **not** `Resume`, waits for the park, asserts `Resume` and the row, releases, and asserts the run finishes; **case 25** asks to pause during the final answer and asserts the run completed **and** the record carries the too-late notice and does **not** claim a park; **real-model case 31** accepts either honest outcome and fails only on silence. **Four falsifications, each reverted and re-run green:** removing the isolation guard fails the throwing-listener test; removing the edge guard fails both "announces nothing" tests; restoring label-flips-on-click fails three render assertions including the one written for that defect; dropping the `parked` payload shape fails the field case. **Known limit, recorded:** a pause still cannot stop a model call already in flight — the fix is honesty, not new power. |
| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bl) The commands button draws a forward slash in a key cap — and until now the button had no coverage at all.** The founder's report had two halves and both were right: *the icon was a backslash*, and *a bare line is hard to recognise*. **The defect was invisible to every check in the repository, and the geometry says why:** the icon was one SVG line from `(8,5)` to `(16,19)`, and **in screen coordinates y runs DOWNWARD**, so it started at the upper left and ended at the lower right — a backslash, the one glyph that means the opposite of what the button does. The markup parsed, the button worked, the tooltip said *Commands*, and **a diagonal line is a diagonal line to every assertion that is not about DIRECTION**. So the new assertions test direction as a PROPERTY, not as numbers: read both endpoints, find the higher one (smaller `y`), require it to be the one with the larger `x` — which survives a redraw with other numbers, reordered endpoints, or a rewrite as a path. **The legibility half is a KEY CAP**, because a lone diagonal reads as a stray stroke and a slash inside a rounded square reads as a key, which is what the affordance is; the slash is asserted **centred** in the box, since a glyph jammed into a corner is the kind of thing nobody writes a test for and everybody sees. **Two values were settled by rendering the alternatives and looking at them**, and the reasoning is not obvious from the numbers: the icon is **16px where the paperclip beside it is 14**, because **a box is mostly empty space and measures lighter than a solid glyph at the same size** — at an equal 14 it read as the FAINTER of the two controls, the opposite of the ask — and the slash **fills the box** (12 of the 16 inner units) after a first attempt at 11 looked lost in the middle. The radius stays 4, matching the button's own. **The durable half is the coverage gap: the commands button had NO render coverage** — not the glyph, not the click — so the suite also pins the two ways an icon swap stops being cosmetic: the accessible name and tooltip, and **the click opening the command menu with rows in it and closing it again**. **Not asserted, deliberately: the icon's colour**, which is `currentColor` on `--pl-muted` like every control in the bar — a theme property with one home, checked by eye on both grounds rather than claimed as tested. | **825 unit tests**, unchanged (shell-only) · render **22 suites / 614 assertions** (+19, in the new `commands button` suite) · `tsc -b --force` clean; bundle + extract-inline + `node --check` PASS, with the new geometry confirmed present in the built bundle · **eight falsifications, each reverted and re-run green:** restoring the original backslash fails the direction assertion — **the suite fails on the exact defect reported**; removing the box fails **6**; moving the slash off-centre fails **1**; shortening it fails the fill assertion; drawing the icon at 13px fails the paperclip comparison and at 8px fails the legibility floor; emptying `aria-label` fails the accessible-name check; breaking the open and the close each fail their own. **Field acceptance NOT re-run, on the handoff's own rule** — it is for changes touching the mode gates, refusals, cards, session memory, plan note, transcript or egress, and an icon inside a button is none of those; the field driver never touches this control either, which was checked rather than assumed. **A process failure is recorded rather than glossed:** the change was reported done with the VSIX never packaged or installed, so the founder reloaded and **still saw the backslash** — the render suite proves a change is correct, and `package` → `install:vsix` → reload is what makes it exist for him. The two were conflated because the field suite's exemption is conditional and the install is not. The packaged VSIX was then unpacked and **read back to confirm the new geometry is inside it**, so what he reloaded into is known rather than assumed. |
| 2026-10-07 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49bk) The mode-change question card is closed, because its answer would be a lie.** D49bj recorded one thing and did not fix it: with `auto` live and reachable, a model could ask the user to loosen the mode mid-run. **The founder read it back and reached the sharper conclusion himself — *"it is only a suggestion then no point showing a question card"* — and the reason is worth stating precisely, because it is worse than a card that does nothing.** **A question card's answer travels back to the model AS A TOOL RESULT.** So a card reading *"switch to auto?"* returns **"Yes"** to a model whose mode is unchanged: **the model is told the user agreed to something that did not happen**, and its next move rests on a permission it does not hold. In `edit` it recovers (the write raises its own card); in `plan` it hits the same wall again. Recoverable, and still the exact failure this codebase keeps removing — a control whose framing implied it would act, leaving a false belief behind. **The card was rejected for creating the belief, not merely for failing to act.** **A correction to this repository's own account, made while answering him:** an earlier statement (mine, in conversation) was that the worker prompt never names the three modes. **That is false** — the prompt's opening paragraph names all three and their meanings. What it never says is **which mode is active**: the mode is not passed into `buildWorkerRole` at all, so the model knows the vocabulary and not where it stands, and learns where it stands one way only — by being refused (`blocked: <tool> is not permitted in plan mode`). **So there is no detection in Plato and no suggestion machinery: there is a refusal, and a model that can read it.** **Why the fix is wording and not a guard:** there is nothing to enforce — the model has no mode tool and core gets a read-only getter, so it CANNOT change the mode; the only defective channel is the wording it chooses. One passage therefore joins the prompt where modes are introduced: the mode is the user's to change and the model cannot change it; if a call comes back refused because of the mode, say in one sentence what you would do if it were permitted and let the user decide; never put a mode change on a question card. **The positive half is as load-bearing as the prohibition** — a rule that only forbade the card would leave a model that hit a refusal with nothing to say, and a refusal is exactly when the user most needs to hear what is blocked. **Named and not taken: make the card real** — the host could accept a mode request and switch on a Yes, which would make the card honest and would hand the model a sanctioned channel for asking to loosen its own permissions mid-run. A posture choice, not a defect, and it stays available. | **825 unit tests** (+3) · render **21 suites / 595 assertions**, unchanged as no UI changed · `tsc -b --force` clean; bundle + extract-inline + `node --check` PASS · **two falsifications, each reverted and re-run green, one per half of the passage:** deleting the card sentence fails **1** assertion, and deleting the statement-plus-instruction fails **2** — so neither half is riding on the other. Field acceptance re-run on this state because **a prompt change alters what a real model is told: 7 of 7 clauses, 23 cases, 30 runs**, with clause 2 (the gates hold, per mode) and clause 5 (the run's account of itself is honest) the clauses nearest the change. |

But three things around that live switch did not hold up, and two of them were silence.** **(1) The mode did not survive a reload.** Neither half stored it — the host started at `edit` and the view started at `edit`, independently — so a window reload, an extension update or a VS Code restart **took the user's choice with nothing said**. This is the identical defect the model picker already had fixed for the model (D49x: *a preference must outlive the panel*), and the mode never got the same treatment; **the two sides agreeing by luck is what hid it**, which is also why storing only one of them would have been worse than storing neither. **(2) A switch left no trace in either record** — the line the user sees was posted to the live view alone, so a reload dropped it, and it was never a bus event, so the transcript never had it. Worse, **no run record named the mode at all, not even the one it began in**: a transcript could not tell a write a card let through from one that ran free in `auto`, and a run in `edit` where nothing happened to be gated was indistinguishable from a run in `auto`. **(3) A card already on screen is not reconsidered** — switching to `plan` while an approval card is up leaves the card live and Allow still writes. **Left as it is, deliberately:** the user is the one clicking and Deny is on the same card, so the fix is wording (the notice now says the change applies from the next request) rather than mechanism. **Auto-answering the card was rejected outright** — a mode click that resolved a pending approval would grant a permission the user never gave for that action, the opposite of the rule the codebase already follows: a card released early is always answered with the SAFE value. **What was built:** the mode is stored beside the log and the title and **validated on the way in**, because a stored value is a boundary and an unrecognised one would otherwise reach the permission table and deny every tool; the host sends it on the restore payload, because the view cannot guess it. **The record now carries the mode twice, from two different truths** — the run header names the mode the run **began** in (a **required** field on `run-started`, so an emitter that forgets it is a compile error rather than a record that cannot say what its own writes were gated by), and a mid-run switch is pushed into the conversation and emitted as a notice on the run's own bus. **The field means "what this run started under" and never "what it ended under"**; one field claimed over the whole run would be wrong about every write made before the switch, which is why the switch gets its own line. `schemaVersion` stays **1** — the field is added, not redefined, and nothing branches on the number. **The known risk, recorded here and addressed by D49bk in the same commit:** `auto` is the wide mode and the switch is live, so a model can write *"this needs several commands — switch to auto"* mid-run and one click removes the card from every remaining write and fetch. **Nothing is bypassed** — the user still clicks — but the model is now a party that can request more permission mid-run. The one design worth copying is **Claude Code's sandbox `mask` mode** — a placeholder reaches the command, the real value is substituted only on the way out to an allowed host. **So: a protected text file reads, with every value replaced by `«masked:NAME»` — the keys, the comments and the line structure survive and the model never holds a value it could move.** Six rules carry it and four are traps found by writing it: **it FAILS CLOSED** (a file whose lines cannot ALL be accounted for is refused, because a multi-line value masked on its first line would pass the rest through verbatim — a keystore, a private key and a one-line blob all land here); **every tool result is scrubbed** through the session's secret map, which closes the hole masking alone leaves open — the model materialises the value into an unprotected file and reads it straight back; **a write un-masks**, and a diff is applied against the MASKED view, because the model's SEARCH strings were written against what it was shown; and **a placeholder the vault has never seen is refused rather than written**, since writing it through produces a config that looks right and authenticates nothing. **No new approval card**: with the value withheld there is nothing to approve, and every SINK already has a gate in the modes that have gates — a write asks in `edit`, a command asks in `edit`, egress asks in `edit` and always in `auto`. `shell` is the one place the old refusal stays, because a command's output cannot be masked before Plato has seen the values — and the message says what the read is for instead. **The protected list is now the owner's**: `plato.secrets.protect` adds names, `plato.secrets.allow` takes exact paths off, and the refusal for an unmaskable file names the setting that would allow it. Two harness traps were found by this work and are recorded in `scripts/field/README.md`: a `seed` written on the case instead of the run fails as an `ENOENT` that reads like a product bug, and **a tool result's 400-character head hid the placeholder** in `local.properties`, ten comment lines above its one assignment, so a masked read was indistinguishable from an unmasked one — the stub now records a `masked` boolean that does not depend on where in the file it falls. |

the hash is what answers *"has this file changed since Plato wrote it"* and what lets an undo **refuse rather than clobber a file the user has since edited** — Kilo Code's reported bug, closed by construction rather than by care. **Three things building it decided that D49ax had not, and each is load-bearing:** the copy is taken **at the executor, not at the approval preview** (in `auto` no preview is built, so capturing there would leave the one mode where undo matters most with nothing to undo — field case 18 runs in `auto` for exactly this reason); the plan is **remade when the user clicks, never held from the card** (the user can edit a file between reading the card and clicking it, and applying a held plan would put the old copy over their work — the same bug returning through a different door); and **a file Plato created is undone by deleting it** — D49ax was silent on this, it is the feature's one destructive action, and it is done **last**, guarded by the same fingerprint, with the card saying *"Plato created this one, so it is removed"* rather than "put back", because a click that deletes a file must not be described as a restore. **Four smaller decisions the build forced, recorded because a silent deviation from a decided design is what this repository keeps having to un-believe:** a corrupt index is **not rebuilt** (D49ax said reconcile should rebuild by walking, and that cannot work — the copies are named `<seq>.bin`, so a walk recovers no path and no hash; the rule that actually matters, *a corrupt index deletes nothing*, is intact and falsified); `bytes` records the size of what was **replaced** and only copied files count toward the cap (the first version reported the file's size NOW in the "too big to undo" message); `formatBytes` dropped a trailing `.0`, because *"above the 1.0 MB limit"* reads like a machine wrote it; and **both levels are reachable**, with "Just the last change" drawn only when the request touched more than one file, since with one file the two actions are identical. **The store is bounded and sweeps itself**, which the founder asked for explicitly: 30 days, 200 MB per project, the most recent 10 requests always kept, **whole requests** evicted oldest-first, dead projects and crash orphans reclaimed at activation. **No user path ever becomes a file name** — the copies are numbered and the index maps the number to the path — so every removal is built from the store root and checked against it with the same `resolveInRoot` the file tools use. The index is written whole and renamed into place, because a half-written index is the one corruption this store cannot recover from. **Cross-platform per the founder's instruction:** one module-level `homePlatoDir()` uses `homedir()` and `join`, so Windows gets `C:\Users\<name>\.plato` with no `~` expanded by hand; containment is `path.relative` + `isAbsolute` rather than a string prefix compare, which would break on Windows' case-insensitivity; stored paths are POSIX and joined back on read. **The report names the terminal commands undo cannot cover**, because in `auto` a command runs unasked and Plato has no record of which files it touched — and it ends with where to look instead. | `undo.test.ts` (31) + `undoCopy.test.ts` (14) + 5 loop tests pinning the seam + the `undo` render suite (43 assertions). **Falsified four times, each reverted:** removing the fingerprint check fails both edited-file refusals; letting a corrupt index through **deletes the store's copies**; `innerHTML` for a file name fails the markup assertion; counting the plan's total instead of what happened fails the honesty assertion. **And three times in a real window** (`node scripts/field/acceptance.mjs --run`: 20 cases, **7 of 7 clauses**), all in `auto`: **case 18** — a created file removed, an edited file back byte for byte, and the undo-of-the-undo returning the edit; **case 19** — the user's own edit written from OUTSIDE Plato between the run and the `/undo`, with the card naming the refused file and saying why BEFORE the click while the other file goes back, and the report naming the `shell` command it cannot cover; **case 20** — the only file refused, so **no card is drawn at all** and the file survives byte for byte. **One bug was found by a photograph and no assertion would have caught it:** case 19's first run reported *"Partly undone. Plato put back 1 file of the 1 it was going to change"* — the wrong verb for a file that had just been REMOVED from the tree, and a denominator counting only the files it meant to act on, so a request that also refused one read as a complete success under a headline saying "partly". Every substring the harness checked was present and the run PASSED. The undo block now takes a shot with the card up and a shot with the report on screen, and the sentence is rebuilt from two counts — what Plato did, with the verb each file actually got, and what it did not. **The verification itself needed a repair, and it is recorded rather than glossed:** a full acceptance run reported *"M4 is NOT met"* and was wrong — one case threw a stale element setting the orchestration mode, its cleanup never ran, Settings stayed on screen covering the New Chat button, and **three cases that had never started were reported as failing**. Fixed in the driver, and because the re-run stayed silent (so the repair never executed) it was **proven by a throwaway probe that put the failure in place on purpose: 4/4 checks, and 2/4 with the fix neutered**. |

| 2026-09-27 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49ax) Undo is decided: Plato's own copies of what it replaced, not a parallel history — Tier C item 3 — and the decision is backed by measurement, not by instinct.** The item was scoped as *"checkpoints / undo — large, and it changes what an approval means."* **Both halves were wrong in useful ways.** It is **small**: the old bytes are already read at the write site for the guard and the approval diff, so capture costs one disk write and **no extra read**. And it **does not change what an approval means** — the write still happens as approved; what changes is what a *mistake* costs. **The founder's instinct was right and the field agrees for a reason he did not name.** Twelve agents were surveyed: four mechanisms ship — a hidden git repo (Cline, Roo, Kilo, Gemini), the user's own repo (**Aider, and only Aider**), per-file copies (Claude Code, Cursor), the editor's undo state (Copilot, Windsurf) — and **Codex REMOVED its undo** because it caused *"pretty significant problems for many users — including those who didn't use the feature"*. **Every reported failure has one shape: the snapshot was bigger than the change.** Cline deleted untracked files outright (#1213) and corrupted real repos with `.git_disabled` (#9286); Roo leaked commits into the **wrong repository** in a dev container (#4567); Kilo, verbatim, *"Restore checkpoint removed all the changes of mine also"* (#1670); Cursor's snapshots grow unbounded; Copilot leaves empty files behind (#9391). Snapshotting a whole workspace to protect one file makes every bug a whole-workspace bug. **The editor's own local history was measured in real VS Code 1.136.0 with a throwaway extension and cannot be the mechanism:** a direct disk write records **nothing** (and that is how Plato writes today, so Plato's edits are invisible to the user's Timeline); the store keeps what the editor **SAVED** rather than what it **FOUND**, so **the pre-Plato state is never recoverable** — and that is the state most often wanted; rapid saves **merge** (10 s window, and Plato's edits are back to back); there is **no API** for it and the entry is not on disk while the window is open. **The line-ending fear recorded before the test did NOT reproduce** — a CRLF file kept CRLF with `files.eol` forced to `\n`, because that setting governs new files — but a worse hazard **is** real and was measured: with the file open and unsaved typing in it, routing through the editor **writes the user's unsaved work to disk**. So Plato writes to disk as before and keeps its own copies. **The design:** store the **old bytes and a fingerprint, never the new bytes**, so the store is a record of what was replaced and **cannot become a second source of truth** about what a file is; the fingerprint is what lets an undo **refuse rather than clobber** a file the user has edited since — Kilo's bug closed by construction — and makes **redo** fall out for free, since a restore is itself a write with a before. **Two limits stated in the message, not hidden:** terminal commands are outside undo because Plato has no record of which files a command touches (Claude Code and Copilot share the hole and never mention it), and a changed file is refused with the rest offered. **Two numbers in the first draft were guesses and both were replaced:** the per-file cap moved **256 KB → 1 MB** (the old bytes are already in memory, so a copy costs no read; 1 MB covers sources and lockfiles, and above it is build output that can be rebuilt), and **the 20-request limit was invented and is the wrong shape** — twenty small requests might be 4 MB while twenty lockfile snapshots could be gigabytes, so the limits are **30 days / 200 MB per project**, with **the most recent 10 requests always kept**. **Cleanup is a requirement, not a follow-up** (founder: *"make sure Plato cleans up after itself properly after limits expire and does not let unbounded storage pile up"*): four leaks, each closed — over-cap and over-age by evicting **whole requests** oldest-first, **deleted projects** by a dead-project sweep (each store records its real workspace path), and **crash orphans** by a reconcile pass; an **index file** makes the cap arithmetic on one small file instead of a walk on the write path, the expensive work runs at activation and end of run, **a corrupt index must delete nothing** (it would name nothing and take the store with it), and **every delete path is confined to the store and verified to be**, because Cline's worst bug is a restore that deleted a user's files. **Related find, and an overstatement of mine corrected:** the run records under `~/.plato/runs/` have **no sweeper at all** — but they are **not** the leak the first draft of this row called them. Measured: **285 runs occupy 1.4 MB**, mean **5 KB per run**, largest 116 KB, after weeks of work including every harness run; at that mean, **50 runs a day for a year is 89 MB raw and 6.4 MB gzipped** (9.2× compression measured on the largest transcript). **The two stores must not share a rule** — undo copies are *derived* data whose loss costs convenience, run records are *primary* data whose loss destroys the only record. So: **compress, do not delete.** Recorded as its own item. | Decision recorded in D49ax and in HANDOFF; **not yet built**. Evidence is the twelve-tool survey and a **throwaway probe extension run inside the harness's real VS Code 1.136.0**, which wrote one file directly to disk and another through the editor API and then read **VS Code's own local-history store off disk** — reports kept at `tmp/history-probe/` (`report-default.json`, `report-gap.json`, `report-eol-lf.json`). The measured results: no entry for a disk write in any run; the pre-Plato content absent from the store in every run; two entries only with the merge window at 1 s and a 12 s gap; and `"userUnsavedWorkCommitted": true` with `userTyped()` in the saved file. Repo left clean (`tmp/` is ignored); no product source changed. **Two pre-existing table defects in this file were also repaired, with no content changed:** one row quoted a literal header-row string inside a code span with its pipes unescaped (6 stray pipes), and **seven rows dated 2026-09-02 were still in the pre-Commit-column shape** — so every cell in them rendered one column out, showing the summary under `Area` and the verification under `Summary`. An empty Commit cell is inserted in each, because the commit is genuinely not recorded; no other character was altered. |

| 2026-09-27 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49aw) A run is now bounded by repetition, not by volume — Tier C item 2 — verified end to end in the harness's own VS Code.** D49ab deleted the turn cap and deliberately replaced it with nothing, so in `auto` nothing stopped a model that kept calling tools without concluding. **The old cap counted the wrong thing** — it cut a *working* run at turn 40 after eleven approved writes — and every bound that survives in the field counts **wasted effort** and **resets the moment anything succeeds**. A *blocked* call does count toward repetition (its message is deterministic text) and not toward no-progress. **Deliberately NOT detected, each for a named false positive:** alternating patterns (edit→build→edit→build is both the normal working cycle and a textbook oscillation), a workspace that has not changed (reading and planning legitimately change nothing), and paraphrased repeats. **Nothing ends a run on its own** — the model is nudged first and only a repeat *after* that is put to the user, because a silent stop cannot be told apart from a finished run; no shipping agent aborts either, and Claude Code has no detector at all. **The messaging was the founder's other condition** and is part of the decision: every sentence names the call through `digestToolCall` (*"Plato has made the same call 3 times (shell: pnpm build)"*) and the no-progress card says **what last worked**, because "the same call repeated 5 times" tells a person nothing. **The escape hatches, since the user must never be left hanging:** Keep going (carrying whatever they typed, which is the fastest unstick), Stop (a clean end with a note that names what happened), **the composer's Stop button, which the card never disables**, typing mid-run, and switching modes; and a run with no way to ask **stops rather than pretending it asked**. Answering "Keep going" exempts that one call for the rest of the run, so it cannot nag while a *different* stuck pattern is still caught. **Two defects found along the way.** The field harness **never compiled core** — the bundle resolves `@applivity/plato-core` through its package `main`, which is BUILT `dist/`, so a core source change was tested against stale code while the run looked green; found because a *falsification refused to fail*. `run-field.mjs` now runs `tsc -b` first. The scanner now models that: an unquoted glob is **still** refused (the load-bearing refusal — `cat *.properties` names no deny-listed file and would read one), a quoted one is allowed, and **a double-quoted `$` or backtick is still refused because those expand even inside double quotes**; single quotes suppress everything. | 2026-09-24 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49an) A read-only shell command now runs in `edit` without a card — as an ALLOWLIST, which is the design decision rather than an implementation detail.** His instruction: *"for read only shell commands the edit should not ask permission."* The reason it matters is attention, not speed: **a run that stops to approve `git status` and `ls` is a run whose approvals the user starts clicking through, and an approval nobody reads protects nothing.** **Why an allowlist and not a "does this command write?" check:** a command is a program, so predicting its text is a guess, and the guess fails in both directions. The question answered instead is narrower and answerable — *is this one of a small set of programs, given only plain words, that cannot write?* — so **anything unrecognised asks as before, and a mistake costs a click instead of a write.** `sort` (has `-o`), `sed` (has `-i`), `awk` (writes from inside its own program), `tee` and every network tool are absent on purpose; `find`, `tree` and `file` are present with their write flags named and refused. **Three ways out, all refused, and the second is load-bearing for the secret rule:** (1) **shell syntax** — `; & \| < > $` backtick, parens, braces, `!` and a newline disqualify a command, so what passes is plain words; (2) **globbing** — `*`, `?`, `[` — because the deny-list matches the file NAMES in the text, so **`cat *.properties` names no secret and would read one**; with globbing refused a command can only open files it spells out, and spelling one out is what the deny-list catches; (3) **leaving the workspace** — a leading `/` or `~`, or any `..` — the containment the file tools already enforce. **The composition is where a widening goes wrong, so it is pinned:** only `ask` becomes `allow`, a `deny` is never upgraded, so **`plan` keeps no shell even for `git status`** — "plan runs nothing" is what makes plan safe, and one exception would turn a rule into a judgement call. The secret refusal runs BEFORE the mode decision, so `cat keystore.properties` is refused outright and **no card appears for it at all** — asserted at the loop level, because the ORDER is what makes it safe. **Deliberately still gated, and worth knowing:** `npm test`, `./gradlew`, `node`, `python3` and every run script — a build file executes arbitrary code and cannot be inferred from the text, so **a run still stops before it runs your tests**; the fix would be a per-project allowlist the user writes, not built because nothing has shown these prompts are the annoyance and `auto` already exists. **Residual risks recorded rather than implied:** a **symlink** defeats the lexical check (the secret rule has the same limit, but it used to be backed by a card the user read); **`git status` is not strictly read-only** (it may refresh `.git/index`); and the per-program flag lists are **audit results, not proofs**. **Also the follow-through on the question card:** the description now asks for three *real* alternatives rather than variations of one, for options the model would genuinely act on, and tells it not to ask when only one answer is plausible — *"a model should really work hard to give us plausible options within limits"* is the founder's sentence and now the tool's text. | `tsc -b --force` clean; | 2026-09-24 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49am) The question card is capped at 3 options and 4 questions — and answering the founder's worry about nagging found a real defect in the session memory.** His instruction: *"6 is too many, lets cap it to 3 choices (radio or checkbox) and one free text box, and maybe may ask up to 4 questions at a time. we don't want overwhelm the users."* The schema went from **2-6 options / 1-3 questions** to **2-3 / 1-4**, and **the limits are stated in prose as well as in the schema**, because a constraint the prompt never mentions is one the model crosses. **The free-text row is not counted** — the card adds it on top, so 3 options are 4 rows. **The cost, stated rather than hidden: 3 is tight for a MULTI-select question**, where listing candidates is the point; it was flagged before the change and the founder chose one number for both, which is also the simpler thing to explain. **The ceiling binds the MODEL, not the user** — a model may offer fewer, the card renders whatever arrives, and nothing truncates; refusing an over-long call was rejected because the card would then be unable to show a question the model had already decided to ask. Pinned by test, since these are product limits. **The second half, found by asking what the nagging actually costs:** the shell decided "is this a write?" with **its own hardcoded pair** (`write_file \|\| apply_diff`) while the mode table had classified `apply_patch` as a write when it landed (D49l) — **so a multi-file patch was the one write tool with NO session memory and raised a card on every call**, even for files already approved that session. That is precisely the nagging he was asking about. **The fix is not adding `apply_patch` to the list but deleting the second list:** `isWriteTool` now derives from `TOOL_KIND`, and a new `writePathsOf` gives the files a write would change, with the rule *ask unless ALL are approved, remember ALL once the user agrees*; a malformed entry is dropped rather than stored as an empty path. **Both live in core, where they can be tested — the shell's lack of tests is why the bug was possible there at all.** | `tsc -b --force` clean; vitest **546/546** (34 files, was 537 — nine new); render **16 suites / 358 assertions, ALL PASS**; bundle + `extract-inline` + `node --check` PASS; VSIX packaged and installed. **Falsified:** restoring the hardcoded pair fails *"says yes to all three write tools, apply_patch included"* with `apply_patch` as the failing case. **Not verified:** the schema limits bind only if the provider enforces JSON Schema on tool inputs; the card renders whatever arrives, so a model that ignores the ceiling still works and simply asks a worse question. |
| 2026-09-24 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49al) A question can now be multi-select: the model sets `multiple: true` and the user gets checkboxes.** His reason, and it is the design: *"a model can reason to ask a multiple choice answer, so we should support checkboxes"* — a model that can see several correct answers and may offer only one forces the user to discard the rest, and the free-text row is a poor workaround because the user ends up retyping the labels. **The change is one optional schema field and one state model.** Four decisions inside it: **(1) the answer is still ONE string per question** — the picks joined by `"; "` — which is what kept the change small, leaving the tool-result transport, the `askUser` signature and the transcript untouched; the accepted cost is that a pick containing `"; "` would be ambiguous, and options are the model's own short labels. **(2) The model is TOLD which kind it asked for:** the tool result reads `Answers:` for a multi question and `Answer:` for a single one, because otherwise a string of labels is indistinguishable from a single answer that happens to contain semicolons. **(3) The shape is the affordance — a box for multi, a circle for single** — and the fill carries the same state, so the reading does not depend on colour. **(4) Free text JOINS the picks rather than replacing them** — the case where "one string per question" could have silently dropped half of what the user said, so it has its own assertion. **One state model for both kinds, deliberately:** the card kept a single answer string, which cannot hold a set, so it now keeps the chosen labels as a list plus the free text and derives the sent string — a second code path for multi was rejected as two restore-on-Back paths, two answered-rules and two Submit gates. **One wording correction, because the first version of this row misled the founder:** the "6" was **not** a multi-select cap. It was the ceiling on how many LABELS the model may offer, single and multi alike, and the card adds the free-text *Others* row **on top** of that — 3 declared labels show as 3 boxes plus a text row — with **no limit on how many boxes may be ticked**. **The label ceiling is now 3, and a call may carry 4 questions, on the founder's instruction — see D49am.** | `tsc -b --force` clean; vitest **537/537** (34 files); render **16 suites / 358 assertions, ALL PASS** (the `question` suite is now **43**, was 28); bundle + `extract-inline` + `node --check` PASS; VSIX packaged and installed. **Falsified, three reverts at once** — a circle for every question, a multi click that replaces instead of toggles, and every question forced multi — **fails 5 assertions** and exits 1. **Inspected as an image:** a headless screenshot of a multi card with two boxes ticked, confirming square-vs-circle and filled-vs-empty read apart. |
| 2026-09-24 | this commit | M5.0 | **(D49ak) Three modes, not four: `act` is merged into `edit`, and `auto` now fetches without asking.** The founder's finding, and correct: **he could not tell `edit` and `act` apart.** They differed where the names do not describe — old `edit` asked for a write and then **remembered it per file for the session** but **denied the shell**, so it was quiet about edits and could run nothing; old `act` let the shell through and asked on **every single write**, so it could run anything and nagged. **The merged mode is strictly better than either it replaces** — `edit`'s per-file memory plus the shell: reads free, writes ask once per file per session, commands ask every time. **The argument that makes it more than tidiness, which the founder did not make:** the old `edit` **denied the shell while the worker prompt REQUIRES verification** (*"mark a step completed only once you have verified it"*), so the mode was asking the model to obey a rule it had no tool for. **`plan` was left with no shell, and that was the one part of his proposal not built as stated** — he asked for a `plan` that could "execute scripts but not change project files", and **a host can classify a tool but not a command** (`rm`, `sed -i`, `git checkout`, `npm install` all write, and sorting them by text is **exactly the mechanism that failed in D49ai**). **Superseded the same day: a read-only ALLOWLIST turned out to be the enforceable form of what he wanted, and `plan` now runs read-only commands (D49an, D49ao).** **Second change, amending D49p: `auto` fetches without asking**, per his instruction — recorded as the risk it is (no card between a fetched page and the model, so an injected instruction can steer the model to a URL carrying data), while three rules stay because they live in the EXECUTOR and not in a permission: no redirect onto a loopback/private address, no non-text response, and fetched content labelled untrusted. **The merge supersedes a contract stated in two documents** (`BLUEPRINT.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md` invariant 3), which stay verbatim per D48 and gained a superseded-map row. **No settings migration: the mode is not persisted**, so no stored `act` can exist to migrate. | `tsc -b --force` clean;
